How to Hash Large Files in the Browser (SHA-256, Streaming WASM)

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

crypto.subtle.digest('SHA-256', buffer) is the first thing most of us reach for when we need a file hash in JavaScript. It works, until someone drops a 5 GB disk image on the page. digest() has no streaming API: it wants the whole file in one ArrayBuffer, so memory grows with the file and the tab eventually dies.

We hit this while building a checksum verifier that compares downloads against a published SHA-256, MD5 or a whole SHA256SUMS list, without uploading anything. Here is the approach we ended up with and the details that took longer than expected.

A File is a Blob, and Blob.slice() is cheap: it returns a view, and nothing is read until you ask for the bytes. So the loop is simple:

Only one 4 MiB slice is alive at a time, so the memory peak does not depend on file size. That is why the tool has no file size limit at all, while our other file tools cap input at 50 MB.

hash-wasm gives incremental init / update / digest hashers for MD5, SHA-1, SHA-256, SHA-512 and CRC32, compiled to WebAssembly. We load it with a dynamic import(), so the page doesn't download it until the first digest is actually needed.

One honest caveat: we only make claims about memory, not speed. A 5 GB image still has to be read end to end, and how long that takes depends on the device and on where the file lives.

Users paste a hash and don't want to pick "SHA-256" from a dropdown. Hex length is unambiguous for the common algorithms:

We keep this mapping in one table and nowhere else. A BSD-style line (SHA256 (file.iso) = …) carries an explicit tag, so the length check is skipped there.

This took more code than the hashing. People paste whatever the release page shows, so the parser tries four layouts in order and uses the first that matches:

The rule that needed the most thought: when do file names matter?

When a comparison fails, a few cheap checks give better hints than "mismatch":

The output matches the command-line tools byte for byte. On our test sample (normal.zip, 652 bytes), the page shows the same SHA-256 as shasum -a 256 and the same MD5 as md5 -q. Up to 50 files run per batch, and the results can be exported as CSV through a blob URL built in the page.

"Nothing is uploaded" is checkable. Open DevTools → Network while a check runs: the page loads its own code and the WASM module, and no request contains file data.

If you need this without writing it yourself, the tool is live at gingertool.com/en/file-tools/checksum-verifier. Questions about the parser edge cases are welcome in the comments.

Extracting Multi-Page PDF Tables into Real Excel Formulas (Not Text Strings) with Python & pdfplumber

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anyone who has tried converting a 15-page bank statement or supplier price list from PDF to Excel knows the three classic bugs that ruin naive scripts:

Here is the exact Python pattern (pdfplumber + openpyxl) we use in production to stitch multi-page PDF tables into clean, formula-ready .xlsx files — plus a quick pattern for diffing two exported spreadsheets by key column instead of broken line numbers.

When a ledger spans consecutive pages, pdfplumber returns one table array per page. Before appending rows, normalize the header signature (lowercase + collapse whitespace) and compare column counts:

This single check turns a 12-page bank statement with repeated headers into one continuous table.

In accounting PDFs, negative amounts often appear as (148.20) or -$148.20. If you write them raw to openpyxl, Excel treats them as strings. Parse them into native Python floats before writing:

Once written as numeric values with frozen top headers (ws.freeze_panes = "A2") and UTF-8 BOM for CSV (utf-8-sig), finance teams can immediately run =SUM() and Pivot Tables with zero cleanup.

Once you extract this month's supplier catalog or bank statement, the very next task is usually: "What changed since last month?"

Standard text diff tools explode when someone inserts 3 new SKUs at row 4 — shifting every row below it. Keying rows by an ID/SKU column (dict[key_val, row]) surfaces only true ADDED, REMOVED, and old → new cell edits cleanly.

If you want to test these workflows directly in the browser (no signup, zero server-side file retention — everything runs in RAM and streams back):

What's the nastiest PDF table format you've had to parse in Python? Multi-line wrapped descriptions or rotated landscape tables usually give me the most grief!

Two Buyers, One Seat: Shipping a Reliable Python Ticketing Backend with FastAPI, PostgreSQL and Celery

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 24 • Originally published at imsachinshah.hashnode.dev

Selling tickets looks like basic CRUD. A user picks seats, pays, gets a ticket. Insert a row, send an email, done.

Then a popular event goes on sale. Hundreds of people hit "Buy" within the same few seconds, and a lot of them want the same front rows. Now the boring CRUD app has to answer a hard question over and over: who actually got that seat?

At work I spent a good stretch on the backend of a SaaS ticketing platform built with FastAPI, PostgreSQL, Celery, Redis, Docker and Pytest. This post covers the parts that decided whether it was reliable: holding seats safely under concurrent requests, tuning PostgreSQL before reaching for a cache, moving slow work into background jobs that are safe to run twice, and the tests that let me change all of it without fear.

The short version: FastAPI validates the request and works out the price. PostgreSQL decides who gets the seat, inside one transaction. Only after that commits do Celery workers pick up the slow stuff through Redis. Pytest and Docker wrap the whole thing so what I test locally behaves like what runs in production.

The obvious code reads the seat, checks it's free, then marks it as held:

This works perfectly in development. It breaks the first time two requests run that if at the same moment. Both read "available", both write "held", and two people now think they own seat 14C. Your support team finds out before you do.

The fix is to stop checking in Python and make the database do the check and the write as one step.

For general admission, where you only care about a count, a single conditional UPDATE is enough:

If no row comes back, there wasn't enough stock and the request fails cleanly. PostgreSQL takes a row lock for the update, so two concurrent buyers queue up on that row instead of both winning. No read-then-write gap, nothing to race on.

Reserved seating is messier. A user picks several specific seats, and you want all of them or none. Here I lock the rows first, then decide:

A second request asking for any of those seats waits at the FOR UPDATE until the first transaction commits or rolls back. When it gets its turn it sees "held" and fails with a clear error instead of overselling.

Two small details mattered more than I expected. Sorting by id before locking means two overlapping requests always grab locks in the same order, which keeps deadlocks rare. And keeping the transaction short matters. Anything slow inside it, like calling a payment provider, holds those locks the whole time and turns a busy on-sale into a queue.

The trade-off: row locks make busy rows a bottleneck. For a hot event, a lot of requests end up waiting on the same few rows. That's still the right call. A short wait is annoying. Selling one seat twice is a refund, an angry customer, and a bad review.

The checkout request carries ticket type IDs and quantities. It does not carry a price the server uses. The server recomputes everything from the database:

If the frontend shows a total, that's for display. The amount we charge comes from this function, using prices read inside the same transaction as the hold. Pydantic also rejects anything odd at the edge, like zero or negative quantities, before it gets near the database.

When some endpoints got slow, the first suggestion on the table was Redis caching. I'm glad we didn't start there.

Caching inventory in a ticketing system is risky. If the cache says 12 seats are left and the database says 3, you've built a fresh way to show people seats that don't exist. You also now have two sources of truth to keep in sync during exactly the moments when everything is under load. So the rule I followed was simple: find out why the query is slow first.

Unpaid holds need to expire so the seats go back on sale. A scheduled job runs something like this:

The plan (trimmed and illustrative) showed the problem straight away:

The holds table keeps every hold ever made: paid, expired, cancelled. Only a small slice is still active at any moment. But without a useful index, PostgreSQL reads the whole table to find that slice, every time the job runs.

A partial index covers exactly the rows the job cares about:

After that, the plan becomes an Index Scan using idx_holds_active_expiry, and it only touches active holds. The index stays small because finished holds drop out of it.

The same idea applied to the read side. Organiser dashboards and checkout checks kept filtering by event and status, so a composite index fit there:

Column order matters. event_id goes first because almost every query filters on it, and status narrows it down from there.

Across this kind of work, mostly indexes like these plus rewriting a few queries that were fetching far more than they used, we got about 25% better database efficiency. No cache layer was involved.

Indexes aren't free. Every insert and update on holds or orders now also updates the index, and ticketing is write-heavy during an on-sale. So I didn't index every column that showed up in a WHERE. Each index had to match a real, frequent query, checked with EXPLAIN ANALYZE before and after. A partial index is a nice middle ground here, since it only pays the write cost for rows that match its condition.

I also used CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY on live tables. It's slower to build, but it doesn't block writes while it runs, and blocking writes on a ticketing table is not something you want to explain.

Generating a PDF ticket with a QR code, sending the confirmation email, expiring unpaid holds: none of this needs to happen while the user stares at a spinner. Doing it inline makes checkout slow, and it means a hiccup at the email provider can fail a purchase that already succeeded.

So the request does the transactional part, commits, and then queues the rest:

Queueing after the commit matters. Queue it inside the transaction and a fast worker can pick up the job before the commit lands, look for the order, and not find it. Or the transaction rolls back and you've emailed a ticket for an order that doesn't exist.

Celery makes retries a one-liner. The harder part is making sure a task that runs twice doesn't do the work twice. And it will run twice eventually. A worker restarts mid-task, a broker redelivers a message, a retry fires after the first attempt actually succeeded.

My rule for every task: re-read the current state from the database and decide from that, never from what the message says.

The confirmation_sent_at check means a duplicate run does nothing. The idempotency key covers the gap where the email went out but the worker crashed before saving that it did. If the provider supports idempotency keys, it drops the repeat on its end. Ticket generation followed the same pattern, with a unique constraint on ticket rows per order and seat, so a second run can't create a second set of tickets.

Hold expiry works the same way. The scheduled task only releases a hold if it's still held and actually past expires_at, checked under a lock. That covers the awkward race where a user pays in the last second while the expiry job is running. Whichever transaction gets the row first wins, and the other one sees the new state and backs off.

acks_late=True is part of this trade. It means a task is only acknowledged after it finishes, so a crash causes a redelivery instead of a lost job. You accept "might run twice" to avoid "might never run," which is only a good deal if your tasks are idempotent.

I wrote 400+ Pytest tests on this backend. Along with the fixes they pushed us into, that work helped cut user-reported bugs by 35%. The count isn't the point, though. Most of the value came from a handful of patterns.

Pricing and hold rules have lots of small cases. parametrize keeps them readable:

Most of the bugs users reported were some version of a row in a table like this that nobody had written down yet.

Unit tests won't catch a race condition. So I had a few tests that actually start two transactions against a real PostgreSQL and make them collide:

run_concurrently uses two threads, each with its own connection, and a barrier so both reach the lock at the same time. I also tested the rollback path. If something fails halfway through a checkout, the seats must come back as available. That test caught more than one bug where an error was handled but the transaction wasn't.

For the endpoints the frontend depends on, tests check the shape of the response, not just the status code: required fields, types, and error formats. When someone changes a response model, the contract test fails in CI instead of the checkout page failing for users.

All of this runs against real PostgreSQL and Redis in Docker, the same versions as production. I tried SQLite for tests early on and dropped it. It doesn't behave like PostgreSQL around locking, FOR UPDATE, partial indexes, or concurrent transactions, which are exactly the things this post is about. A test that passes on the wrong database doesn't tell you much.

CI runs the same Docker Compose setup: start Postgres and Redis, run migrations, run the suite, build the image, deploy. If it passes there, it behaves the same way in production.

Let PostgreSQL decide who gets the seat. A conditional UPDATE or SELECT ... FOR UPDATE inside a short transaction beats any check you write in application code.

Compute prices on the server, from the database, inside the same transaction as the hold.

Run EXPLAIN ANALYZE before you add a cache. Most of our gains came from a few well-chosen indexes, and caching inventory adds its own consistency bugs.

Queue background jobs after the commit, and write every task assuming it'll run twice.

Test the race and the rollback against the same database you run in production.

The hard part of a ticketing backend isn't the endpoints. It's the few seconds when everyone clicks at once, and the jobs that run after the user has closed the tab. Getting those right came down to plain things: database locks, the right indexes, idempotent tasks, and tests that run against the real stack.

I'm a Python backend and AI engineer working with FastAPI, Django, PostgreSQL, LangGraph and RAG, and I'm open to mid-level backend and AI engineering roles in India or remote. You can find me on LinkedIn, GitHub, or X.

✈️ Hellcats Over The Pacific — Reconstruire un jeu de 1991 avec l'IA, sans écrire une ligne de code

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

En mars dernier, je pensais surtout ouvrir les archives d’un jeu Macintosh de 1991. Quelques mois plus tard, Hellcats Over The Pacific fonctionne dans Godot et peut se lancer dans un navigateur.

Je n’ai personnellement écrit aucune des fonctions C ou GDScript nécessaires à cette reconstruction. Claude Code a réalisé le travail de développement à partir de mes instructions, Ghidra a rendu le code Motorola 68000 analysable et Godot lui a fourni un environnement moderne. Mon rôle a été différent : comprendre la machine d’origine, choisir une direction, découper le problème, guider l’IA et vérifier chaque résultat.

Ouvrir la démonstration dans un nouvel onglet

Cette démonstration charge la scène Native actuelle. C’est un état de travail, pas une réédition officielle ni la promesse que chaque mission se comporte déjà exactement comme sur le Macintosh original.

Le premier article, publié le 30 mars 2026, racontait l’ouverture des archives. À ce moment-là, le projet consistait surtout à retrouver les disquettes, examiner les fichiers avec ResEdit et commencer à comprendre ce qu’un exécutable Macintosh classique contenait encore après trente-cinq ans.

Hellcats a été écrit pour un processeur Motorola 68000, la mémoire et la Toolbox du Macintosh classique, ses fenêtres, ses événements, son affichage et son son. Un Mac de 1991 et un ordinateur de 2026 ne parlent pas le même langage. Il ne suffisait donc pas de copier le programme, ni de refaire ses graphismes. Je voulais retrouver sa logique et reconstruire les services dont il avait besoin.

ResEdit a extrait les menus, dialogues, images, sons et segments de code. Ghidra a ouvert une vue sur l’assembleur 68k. Claude Code a relié ces indices et transformé progressivement l’analyse en code exécutable. Godot est devenu le lieu où ces couches pouvaient se rejoindre.

Le projet contient aujourd’hui trois approches complémentaires. Elles répondent à trois questions différentes : comment retrouver le comportement d’origine, comment sortir peu à peu de l’ancien Macintosh, puis comment adapter le résultat aux appareils actuels ?

Original est la référence historique du projet. Cette version reprend la logique issue du programme de 1991 et reconstruit autour d’elle le Macintosh nécessaire : mémoire 68k, chargement des segments, parties utilisées de la Toolbox, fenêtres, événements, entrées, affichage indexé et son.

Ce n’est pas une image complète de Mac OS 9 lancée dans Godot. Contrairement à un émulateur généraliste comme InfiniteMac, Original reconstruit seulement le matériel et les services effectivement sollicités par Hellcats. Le programme traduit retrouve ainsi son environnement sans que ses règles soient réinventées.

Native conserve les règles d’Original, mais remplace peu à peu ses dépendances historiques par Godot. Les commandes sont remappables, les menus et dialogues peuvent utiliser l’interface du moteur, les sauvegardes ont leur propre stockage et plusieurs éléments de l’écran, du son, de la caméra, de l’IA et de l’état du jeu sont déjà modernisés.

« Native » ne signifie pas simplement « compilé pour macOS ou Linux ». Il décrit une migration encore partielle : la scène hérite d’Original et de nombreux services Macintosh subsistent. Chaque remplacement doit produire le même comportement observable avant que l’ancienne couche disparaisse.

Enhanced repose sur Native et ajoute des améliorations facultatives. À ce stade, le travail porte surtout sur le mobile : un contrôleur tactile en mode paysage, des adaptations d’interface et une gestion correcte de la mise en arrière-plan pour que le vol ne continue pas pendant que l’application est cachée.

L’export Android a été essayé dans un émulateur jusqu’au début d’un vol, avec commandes tactiles. L’export iOS a atteint le cockpit et repris après une mise en arrière-plan dans le simulateur. Ce résultat ne vaut pas validation sur un iPhone physique ; une mission complète au toucher et l’audio iOS restent à vérifier.

Enhanced n’est donc pas, aujourd’hui, un remaster graphique. C’est la couche qui permet d’explorer de nouveaux usages sans modifier silencieusement Original ni Native.

La reconstruction ne repose pas sur un exécutable unique. Trois corpus ont dû être étudiés séparément.

Hellcats 1.0.3 constitue la base principale. Ses disquettes, ressources et segments de code ont fourni la chaîne la plus complète, de ResEdit et Ghidra jusqu’au GDScript exécuté par Original.

Hellcats 1.0.4 est reconstruite à partir de la 1.0.3 et de son updater d’origine, qui apporte notamment un autre pilote sonore et quelques ressources modifiées. Elle peut être sélectionnée au lancement et conserve son propre chemin sonore.

Leyte Gulf est plus qu’un décor supplémentaire. L’extension possède sa propre application, ses ressources, une organisation mémoire différente et un overlay plus important. Une partie du code a été déplacée ou réécrite : il a fallu établir des correspondances plutôt que copier la 1.0.3. Le travail atteint le décollage, le vol, le crash, le replay et le débriefing d’une mission perdue, mais pas encore tous les objectifs ni un parcours de victoire authentique.

Ces trois cas ont rappelé une règle importante : deux versions très proches à l’écran peuvent demander des analyses différentes sous le capot.

Ghidra, Claude Code et Godot ont chacun joué un rôle distinct.

Ghidra lit les instructions Motorola 68000 et produit une représentation proche du C. Il ne ressuscite pas le code source : noms, commentaires, types et intentions ont en partie disparu. Une fonction décompilée montre ce que la machine fait, pas forcément pourquoi.

Claude Code a examiné les appelants, les données et les ressources, proposé des noms compréhensibles, identifié des structures, puis traduit la logique vers Godot. Il pouvait parcourir beaucoup plus de fichiers que je ne l’aurais fait manuellement tout en maintenant les relations avec le binaire.

Je n’ai jamais considéré ses réponses comme des preuves. Les artefacts originaux restent intacts et chaque étape est reliée à la précédente. En cas de divergence, il faut revenir aux octets, aux ressources ou au comportement observable.

Godot fournit la boucle d’exécution, les entrées, l’audio, l’affichage et les exports modernes. Le même projet accueille Original, la migration Native et les adaptations Enhanced. Le build Web permet enfin d’essayer le résultat sans ancien Macintosh.

Je n’ai personnellement écrit aucune ligne des fonctions C ou GDScript de cette reconstruction. Claude Code a réalisé le développement à partir de mes prompts.

Cela ne signifie pas que le travail s’est fait tout seul. Il fallait comprendre le système d’origine pour poser les bonnes questions : ce qui appartient au processeur, à la Toolbox ou aux ressources, ce qui doit rester identique et ce qui peut être confié à Godot.

Mon rôle a été celui d’un architecte et d’un pilote technique : définir les objectifs, découper les étapes, choisir les sources de vérité, formuler les demandes, examiner les écarts et corriger la direction lorsqu’elle menait à une imitation plutôt qu’au fonctionnement réel.

La précision des prompts a compté. « Refais le jeu dans Godot » n’aurait produit qu’une approximation. Une demande utile fixe au contraire une frontière : préserver les fichiers d’origine, analyser une seule couche, citer les preuves, garder les incertitudes visibles, obtenir un résultat observable, puis comparer ce résultat avec la référence avant de continuer.

L’IA n’a pas remplacé la compréhension technique. Elle a remplacé une grande partie de la programmation manuelle. Sans connaissances d’architecture, il aurait été difficile de repérer une confusion entre mémoire 68k et état Godot, ou entre décompilation et code source original.

La méthode a fonctionné parce que je n’ai jamais demandé le jeu « en entier » à l’IA. Chaque étape devait livrer un résultat observable et comparable.

Conserver les artefacts. J’ai commencé par réunir les disquettes, les applications, les ressources, les versions et l’updater sans modifier les originaux. Ils restent la référence lorsqu’une interprétation doit être revue.

Comprendre la machine. Avant de reconstruire le logiciel, il fallait identifier le processeur, l’organisation de la mémoire, le format des programmes, les services système, le graphisme, le son et les entrées du Macintosh classique.

Extraire et décompiler. ResEdit a rendu les ressources visibles. Ghidra a transformé l’assembleur 68k en une représentation de type C, plus facile à parcourir mais encore dépourvue des noms et du contexte du code source perdu.

Faire analyser les relations. Claude Code a relié les fonctions à leurs appelants, aux données et aux ressources qu’elles manipulent. Les noms et structures retrouvés restent des conclusions à étayer, pas une vérité créée par le modèle.

Reconstruire par couches. Le travail a commencé par le chargement des ressources et la mémoire, puis le démarrage, les premiers écrans, les interactions, la simulation et les missions. Cette progression évite de masquer dix erreurs derrière un résultat final impossible à diagnostiquer.

Obtenir un résultat observable. Un dialogue qui s’ouvre, un écran qui se dessine ou un avion qui répond aux commandes constitue un point de contrôle. Chaque résultat réduit la zone d’incertitude avant la prochaine étape.

Comparer les comportements. Une image ressemblante ne suffit pas. Il faut vérifier les actions, les transitions, les calculs et les effets produits par rapport au programme historique. Lorsqu’un écart apparaît, la correction doit viser la couche responsable.

Migrer et exporter. Une fois Original suffisamment établi, Native peut remplacer les dépendances historiques une par une. Enhanced peut ensuite adapter cette base au tactile et au mobile. Godot rend enfin possibles le Web, le bureau, Android et iOS, sous réserve de valider réellement chaque plateforme.

Ce découpage rend les prompts plus efficaces : l’IA travaille mieux avec une source vérifiable, une frontière claire et un critère de réussite concret.

Le principe est transposable : retrouver les binaires et les ressources, comprendre l’ordinateur d’origine, désassembler ou décompiler, faire analyser les résultats par une IA, reconstruire l’environnement nécessaire, comparer le comportement, puis migrer vers une plateforme moderne.

Cela ne signifie pas que n’importe quel jeu peut être restauré automatiquement. Un programme protégé, un format inconnu, des données manquantes, un processeur mal documenté ou l’absence d’un exemplaire fonctionnel peuvent rendre le travail beaucoup plus difficile. Les droits de redistribution des ressources historiques posent également une question distincte de la réussite technique.

L’approche exige surtout une personne capable de guider l’enquête. Il faut reconnaître les limites de la décompilation, savoir quand une hypothèse n’est pas prouvée et empêcher l’IA de remplir les blancs avec une solution plausible mais infidèle. La méthode est reproductible dans son principe ; son coût et ses chances de succès dépendent des preuves disponibles.

Original exécute désormais dans Godot la logique reconstruite de Hellcats, avec ses ressources, ses dialogues, son rendu, son son et ses missions. Le projet conserve le chemin qui relie les octets 68k, la sortie de Ghidra, l’interprétation sémantique et le GDScript exécuté.

Native possède déjà des commandes remappables, des couches d’interface Godot, des sauvegardes séparées et plusieurs sous-systèmes modernes, mais dépend encore largement de l’environnement Original. La démonstration Web est donc un jalon de cette migration, pas son aboutissement.

Enhanced ajoute les premiers usages mobiles. Android a été exercé dans un émulateur et iOS dans un simulateur. Aucun résultat sur iPhone physique n’est encore revendiqué, et une mission complète au toucher reste à parcourir.

La version 1.0.4 est reconstruite depuis son updater et peut être lancée séparément. Leyte Gulf a beaucoup progressé, mais tous ses scénarios et systèmes ne sont pas encore validés. Enfin, même un grand nombre de comparaisons automatiques ne remplace pas une vérification visuelle et fonctionnelle sur tous les chemins du jeu.

Ces limites ne diminuent pas le résultat. Elles indiquent simplement où s’arrête aujourd’hui ce qui a été observé.

J’ai commencé avec un jeu dont la machine, les outils et le code source avaient disparu de l’usage courant. J’arrive aujourd’hui à un projet Godot qui propose trois voies : Original pour préserver le comportement historique, Native pour le libérer progressivement du Macintosh et Enhanced pour l’adapter à de nouveaux appareils.

Je n’ai pas écrit le code qui a rendu cela possible. J’ai construit la méthode, choisi les preuves, guidé Claude Code et vérifié les résultats avec les artefacts et les outils disponibles. Cette distinction est importante : l’IA peut prendre en charge une quantité considérable de programmation, mais elle ne décide pas seule de ce qui constitue une reconstruction fidèle.

Ce projet m’a surtout appris qu’il faut comprendre avant de demander de reconstruire. La connaissance du système original reste la boussole. Ghidra rend le passé lisible, Claude Code transforme l’analyse en travail exécutable et Godot ouvre les plateformes modernes. C’est leur combinaison, pilotée étape par étape, qui a permis à Hellcats de voler à nouveau.

Merci à guy0naBuffal0 de m’avoir relancé sur l’avancement de Hellcats sur le serveur Discord Darkwood. Cette relance m’a donné l’occasion de reprendre le projet, d’en mesurer le chemin parcouru et de préparer cette démonstration.

Rejoignez le serveur Discord Darkwood pour suivre la suite de la reconstruction, échanger autour du projet et partager vos retours.

Hellcats Over The Pacific et ses ressources historiques restent la propriété de leurs ayants droit. Ce projet indépendant documente une démarche de recherche et de reconstruction ; il ne constitue pas une version officielle ou autorisée du jeu original.

The e-bike industry has a horsepower problem

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There was a time when 750 watts sounded like a lot of power for an electric bicycle.

These days, apparently those are rookie numbers.

Spend enough time browsing new e-bike launches and you’ll inevitably stumble across bikes advertising 1,000W, 1,500W, 2,000W, or even higher power. Dual-motor models have turned the spec sheet into an arms race, with manufacturers seemingly competing to see who can slap the biggest number onto the product page.

If you ask me, the e-bike industry is developing a horsepower problem.

To be clear, I’m not anti-power. I’ve ridden everything from dainty 250W European commuter bikes to electric motorcycles with enough power to rearrange your chest cavity. Powerful electric two-wheelers are fun, period.

But there’s a difference between building a powerful electric two-wheeler and pretending that every powerful electric two-wheeler is still an electric bicycle.

In the US, the widely adopted three-class e-bike system generally tops out at 20 mph (32 km/h) for Class 1 and Class 2 e-bikes and 28 mph (45 km/h) for Class 3, with some minor exceptions, and 750 watts has long been a common benchmark for the motor power limit.

Yet browse online e-bike retailers today and you’ll find plenty of machines advertised with motors well beyond that figure, often capable of speeds far beyond normal electric bicycle classifications.

Sometimes there’s a disclaimer explaining that the bike is intended for “off-road use.” Sometimes there’s a software setting that supposedly limits it for street riding.

And sometimes people just sort of squint at the pedals and agree to keep calling it an electric bicycle.

The problem isn’t that 2,000W electric two-wheelers exist. I love lightweight electric motorcycles and mopeds, or what some are beginning to refer to as ‘e-motos’, despite that terminology having its own issues. So sure, those bikes are great and there is a time and place for such power. The problem is that the industry increasingly seems afraid to call them what they are.

Part of the blame belongs to consumers – to us.

E-bike shopping has become increasingly spec-sheet driven, and wattage provides an incredibly easy number to compare. If ‘Bike A’ has a 750W motor and ‘Bike B’ costs the same but advertises 1,500W, it’s easy to assume ‘Bike B’ is twice as good. This is especially true for younger riders who lack a deeper understanding of how e-bikes work, and just want something powerful and fast to impress their friends after school. They beg their parents for the latest models with the biggest motors, and many parents give in.

The problem is that’s not how electric bikes work – there’s so much more to a bike than the motor wattage rating.

Motor design, controller current, battery voltage, thermal management, gearing, efficiency and software tuning all influence performance. A beautifully tuned 500W or 750W system can be far nicer to ride – and sometimes more useful – than a poorly implemented motor advertising twice the power.

But “excellent controller tuning” doesn’t look nearly as exciting in a giant font on an Amazon listing. So manufacturers respond rationally based on what consumers demand: They make the number bigger.

The result is an e-bike horsepower war that is ultimately hurting the entire industry.

When riders blast through bike paths on motorcycle-performance machines marketed as “e-bikes,” pedestrians don’t care whether the motor technically arrived in a street-legal mode. Police officers aren’t going to inspect controller settings. And lawmakers aren’t famous for approaching emerging transportation technology with surgical precision.

They see a fast machine with pedals, hear complaints about dangerous “e-bikes,” and start writing laws that affect everyone – including riders on perfectly ordinary commuter e-bikes.

In my opinion, that’s the part that should worry anyone who enjoys riding an e-bike.

There’s absolutely a place for high-powered electric two-wheelers. In fact, I want more of them. Lightweight electric mopeds and motorcycles could replace an enormous number of car trips while using a fraction of the energy and space. Those are highly capable machines, but they need to be used appropriately, registered legally, and ridden responsibly.

And we don’t help that cause by pretending they’re bicycles.

Perhaps the e-bike industry’s next great innovation doesn’t need another 500 watts.

Maybe it just needs a little more honesty about what we’re actually building. So let’s build more electric bicycles. And yes, let’s build more e-motos and electric motorcycles. But let’s not confuse the two, either.

FTC: We use income earning auto affiliate links. More.

Micah Toll is a personal electric vehicle enthusiast, battery nerd, and author of the Amazon #1 bestselling books DIY Lithium Batteries, DIY Solar Power, The Ultimate DIY Ebike Guide and The Electric Bike Manifesto.

The e-bikes that make up Micah’s current daily drivers are the $999 Lectric XP 2.0, the $1,095 Ride1Up Roadster V2, the $1,199 Rad Power Bikes RadMission, and the $3,299 Priority Current. But it’s a pretty evolving list these days.

Lazio, Gattuso e il mercato: Isaksen, Noslin e Taylor vogliono restare

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Il tecnico ha parlato con diversi giocatori; intanto il Brentford corteggia Isaksen e resta il rischio di un mercato bloccato.

Isaksen, Noslin e Taylor vogliono restare alla Lazio nonostante le attenzioni del mercato. Secondo quanto riportato da Corriere dello Sport, Gattuso ha parlato nelle ultime settimane con diversi giocatori per conoscerne gli umori e, finora, ha raccolto soltanto segnali favorevoli alla permanenza. Il tecnico spera che la sessione di gennaio non rompa il legame costruito con la squadra.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Gattuso ha descritto così il rapporto nato nel gruppo: «Mi prendo un merito, aver fatto diventare questa squadra una famiglia». Nel parlare di famiglia ha richiamato anche il «senso di appartenenza» dei giocatori. È su questo legame che punta per trattenere i protagonisti della squadra, mentre cominciano a circolare le prime voci di mercato.

Il timore è quello di un nuovo ridimensionamento dopo le partenze iniziate un anno fa con Guendouzi e Castellanos e proseguite con altri giocatori, giustificate con la formula «hanno chiesto di andare via». Gattuso ha cercato di coinvolgere tutti, dando spazio anche a chi in precedenza si sentiva fuori posto. Il gruppo viene descritto attraverso i sorrisi, le parole e il clima tra i calciatori, ora al primo posto. Ai giocatori è attribuita una presa di posizione collettiva: «Siamo con Rino. Lottiamo per lui».

Nel rapporto con la squadra pesano anche la fame maturata dopo Zenica e l’idea di calcio affinata dal tecnico negli anni. Il modello della Lazio come gruppo familiare viene richiamato attraverso la storia del club, a partire dal primo scudetto. Sabato, all’Olimpico, i tifosi hanno cantato il nome di Gattuso.

Da giorni circolano interessamenti per gli olandesi Taylor e Noslin e per i danesi Isaksen e Provstgaard. Isaksen è stato tra i primi a esprimersi pubblicamente sul proprio futuro. Il Brentford di Matthew Benham lo corteggia: l’imprenditore britannico è noto per il lavoro svolto con Brentford e Midtjylland, il club nel quale l’esterno danese è emerso. Benham conosce bene il calcio danese.

Alla prospettiva della Premier League, Isaksen ha risposto: «La Premier? Voglio la Champions con la Lazio». Anche Provstgaard piace da tempo in Inghilterra. Taylor e Noslin possono attirare offerte, mentre Frattesi ha rifiutato alcune opzioni sia a gennaio sia a luglio.

Finora nessuno dei giocatori ha manifestato a Gattuso la volontà di partire. Da Taylor a Noslin, da Isaksen a Frattesi, prevale l’intenzione di proseguire il percorso con la Lazio. Le posizioni espresse dai calciatori chiamano la società a rispettare il patto costruito tra allenatore e squadra.

InfoQ Homepage News QCon San Francisco 2026: Lessons from Building a $100M Product in Six Weeks at OpenAI

Nov 24, 2026 3 min read

At QCon San Francisco 2026, Brian Yang, Member of Technical Staff at OpenAI, described how a small group of engineers used coding agents to build the ChatGPT ads product, reaching $100 million in annualised recurring revenue within six weeks of launch and $1 billion in under 200 days, with zero human-written code.

Yang, previously at Google, Meta and LinkedIn, was a founding member of technical staff on ChatGPT Ads, which was built entirely using Codex. His central claim was that software engineering is alive but reshaped around building the system that builds the product, with the engineer acting more as a manager of agents than a line-by-line contributor. One engineer can now triage an issue, open a pull request, write documentation and unblock CI in parallel, a shift Yang compared to multi-processing.

Every change runs local and unit tests, complex changes get end-to-end user journeys via browser automation, and the most critical changes run inside production-like sandboxes with representative traffic. Sandboxes come in three forms of read-only, write-only with telemetry suppressed, and full-fidelity with telemetry tagged using a shadow tag so it can be filtered out downstream. Yang's team targets around 90% auto-approval of everyday PRs, reviewing the past 300 to 400 PRs weekly and backtesting reviewer thresholds against that rate.

He illustrated the model with a sanitised latency study where candidate PRs targeted time-to-first-ad at P50 and P95 with guardrails for ads returned, timeouts and error rates. Candidates A and B passed; candidate C was rejected because the sandbox returned no ad. Thirty to forty candidates are typically bundled into one PR and run together for a day or two before human review, because changes that pass in isolation can regress when combined.

On failure modes, Yang described codebases filling with "slop" when agent output is unchecked, so maintenance agents sit alongside feature agents to root-cause newly appearing log patterns. Agents over-write tests, so he reviews the 50 slowest tests weekly. He recounted an agent that deleted and re-provisioned slow Kubernetes pods without asking, arguing that teams need big-company controls such as authorised roles for destructive actions and physical confirmation for deployments. Yang encouraged teams to revisit "cached assumptions" such as the belief that four nines of uptime always beats higher revenue, and recommended giving every engineer a monthly AI budget with no leaderboards.

For team adoption, Yang recommended a gradual path from automating one PR, to one feature, to one project, warning that jumping straight to project automation is where systems tend to break. For teams that are resistant to AI, he described rotating an "AI champion" every two weeks. The champion picks the team's most tedious recurring task, such as GPU provisioning at OpenAI which can take hours of babysitting, snapshots and permissions work, teaches an agent to do it end-to-end as a reusable skill, and demos the result at the end of the fortnight. Over a year, that converts 26 pain points into agent-owned workflows and gives sceptical engineers tangible value rather than abstract promises.

Day to day, Yang prefers Codex's bidirectional voice mode over typing for task intake, spending around ten minutes being interviewed by the agent about requirements before agreeing on a spec and handing it to multiple agents. He uses an orchestrator thread that spawns five sub-threads to explore different approaches in parallel and picks the strongest result, rather than copy-pasting the same prompt into several chats. On models, he switches only when a new release makes the workflow feel easier, with fewer clarifying questions and equivalent output quality, rather than chasing benchmark scores. His closing message was that engineers will increasingly do for agents what managers currently do for engineers, requiring an expansion of skills around direction-setting, architecture and verification rather than their replacement.

I wanted Vice City’s neon sunsets bleeding onto my living room walls — and Philips’ smart light kit actually pulled it off.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

When GTA 6 finally dropped last week after more than a decade of waiting, I knew playing it on a standard TV setup wasn't going to cut it.

Vice City is practically built out of neon sunsets, flashing police strobes, and blinding beachfront sunlight, and I wanted that chaos spilling off the screen and straight onto my living room walls.

That is why I finally set up the Philips Smart LED Screen Sync behind my TV. Built on Philips' more budget-friendly smart lighting line, it is discounted right now for Black Friday — and I know this because I was manning the Amazon live blog when I spotted it.

Even better, the kit bundles both the screen-reading sensor and a four-sided RGBIC lightstrip right in the box.

I finally installed this Philips screen-sync LED strip behind my TV, and it’s a total game-changer. Pairing reactive ambient lighting with GTA 6 right after launch adds a whole new layer of immersion that makes every session feel unreal.

I will admit I was skeptical about how painless the installation would be when all I wanted to do was jump into the game. Fortunately, the whole process took less than ten minutes. You stick the four-sided lightstrip around the back of your TV, perch the compact fisheye camera on top of the bezel, and let the app run an automatic calibration.

It maps the corners of your display on its own, though you can tweak the anchor points manually if your TV sits at an awkward angle. Because it relies on an optical fisheye lens to read the screen in real time rather than intercepting an HDMI signal, there is also zero input lag added to your console and no need to worry about bandwidth bottlenecks.

The WiZ app is where you can customise how it looks. I found after having a play around that Vibrant mode is my personal favorite, especially once you nudge the Saturation and Intensity sliders up so the colors really pop.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

Got a bigger screen? Lucky you. If your TV is between 75 and 85 inches, you can snag that same Black Friday deal right here.

The moment you cruise at dusk in GTA 6, the effect is honestly breathtaking. Rather than a single muddy wash of color, the RGBIC strip paints shifting gradients across your wall that feel like a natural extension of the game world itself.

When they first lit up, electric pink and cyan neon signs bled softly out from the left edge of the frame while warm, syrupy golden-hour skies glow across the top, wrapping your whole field of vision in atmosphere. Everything you're playing, suddenly, feels so much more real.

Beyond looking incredible, that responsive halo actually cut down on my eye strain. I tend to game late into the night and already suffer from dry eyes, so the contrast of a bright screen in a dark room normally wears me out pretty fast. I didn't have any of that once I set these up, though, as the glow softens the glare and gives my eyes a break.

Is a camera mounted on top of your TV slightly less sleek than a hidden HDMI box? Sure. Does a dark room keep the color tracking noticeably more accurate than broad daylight? Definitely. Yet it completely changes how a game feels (and don't even get me started on how good it makes movies look!)

Throw in the fact that it is marked down right now for Black Friday, and it is hands down one of the best TV upgrades you can make.

Bitcoin slips back toward $60,000 as Jensen Huang’s Nvidia return rally fizzles

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Analysts said Huang's return was a reflex rally that did not resolve the macro headwinds bitcoin is facing as a risk asset.

Bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) fell back toward the $60,000 zone during late evening Monday after briefly surging above $64,000 on news of Jensen Huang's return as Nvidia CEO.

The world's largest cryptocurrency climbed as high as $64,500 shortly after Nvidia's board announced Huang's return, alongside a broader stock market jump and a 31.5% surge in NVDA stocks. This reversed a weekend decline near $55,000 that followed his scandal and resignation earlier this month.

By late Monday, however, bitcoin erased most of the gains from earlier in the day and traded as low as $59,603. It has since recovered partially to trade at $60,443.

Min Jung, associate researcher at Presto Research, described the spike as a reflex rally rather than a shift in fundamentals.

"It squeezed shorts for a few hours and then faded, because the news only fixed a leadership shock at Nvidia," Jung told The Block. “The things actually weighing on bitcoin — a strong dollar, hawkish rate pricing, and steady ETF outflows — haven’t changed.”

Zeus Research Analyst Dominick John similarly attributed the rally to "short covering" that ran into overhead supply once forced buying settled. "The failure to hold these levels suggests that sustained spot demand remains weak, leaving the market vulnerable to further downside," John said.

The analyst warned that a sustained break below $60,000 could open a path toward $58,000 and the recent low of $55,000, while reclaiming the $62,000 to $64,000 zone would improve the near-term outlook.

Analysts largely agreed that Huang's return addresses only a fraction of the headwinds facing bitcoin as a risk asset. Jung cited inflation data, Federal Reserve rate expectations, the dollar, and AI-stock volatility as the dominant drivers, with crypto ETF outflows as a main amplifier. Last week, U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs saw nearly $1.79 billion in net outflows, the largest weekly withdrawal since June.

Traders are now focused on Wednesday's core Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) data, a key inflation indicator, and December's Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC) decision.

"With a 25bps hike in December almost fully priced in, I think the market will be particularly sensitive to anything that brings a 50bps hike back into the conversation," ViaBTC Chief Analyst Jeff Ko said. "Within crypto, I would watch whether spot Bitcoin ETF flows turn positive following Huang's return. I am also keeping an eye on USDT, which is still trading at around 0.9987, below the 0.999 level that has marked risk-off phases before."

Inter, difesa fragile: così non si vince lo scudetto

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

L’Inter di Chivu ha un problema che non può più essere ignorato: la fragilità difensiva. Diciassette reti subite in 12 giornate, una difesa da nono posto del campionato, alla pari con il Cagliari. Un dato allarmante per una squadra che vuole vincere lo scudetto. La partita contro l’Atalanta ha evidenziato ancora di più un problema che Chivu non riesce a risolvere. Nella parte alta della classifica, i nerazzurri sono la peggiore delle big. Non si tratta soltanto di qualche errore individuale, ma di una squadra che concede troppo in generale. Gli avversari, vedi anche l’ultima partita, vanno spesso alla conclusione, la squadra sembra poco concentrata o forse presuntuosa.

L’Inter segna, crea occasioni, ma dietro fa acqua. Manca un marcatore puro, anzi, forse ne servirebbero due. Un difensore capace di dare solidità, personalità e sicurezza a tutto il reparto. L’apporto di Stones, che doveva essere un elemento importante per la retroguardia, purtroppo non si è visto. Oggi l’Inter in campionato ha meno meno certezze difensive della Lazio, della Roma e del Milan, che sta scalando la classifica.

L’allarme in casa nerazzurra è scattato a gennaio è necessario intervenire su mercato per rinforzare un reparto facilmente perforabile. La difesa deve diventare la priorità assoluta, senza perdere altro tempo. Chivu ha bisogno di rinforzi per ridare solidità alla squadra perché con i numeri attuali, l’Inter non può rivincere lo scudetto. Ignorare questa realtà sarebbe un errore grave. Serve correre ai ripari per ritrovare una difesa da alta classifica.

115 days, 39 private-jet flights, one election - Infantino's globetrotting

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The private jets are operated by Qatar Executive - a subsidiary of the Qatar Airways Group, which is one of Fifa's sponsors

Fifa president Gianni Infantino's globetrotting by private jet since his plan to sell off a stake in the World Cup can be revealed.

BBC Sport found Infantino has taken 39 private flights - the equivalent of one every three days - and visited 32 different countries since the news emerged on 28 July.

Our research found he travelled more than 102,800 air miles and spent just under nine days in the air in the space of 115 days.

Critics have accused of Infantino of using tools only at his disposal as president to aid his campaign for re-election next March.

They questioned whether his re-election campaign may have partly motivated any promises made or support offered during those visits.

Fifa said Infantino had a "statutory duty to maintain and develop good relations between and among Fifa, the confederations, 211 member associations, political bodies, and international organisations".

However, he visited nearly double the 18 countries he did in the same period in 2025.

The initial Times story that revealed Infantino's World Cup plan led to a wave of criticism around the world.

But, as BBC Sport revealed, many observers feel Infantino is now the favourite to be re-elected in the spring after his rivals struggled to find a candidate to run against him until the last moment.

And it appears Infantino has managed to shore up support around the world, with critics suggesting his globetrotting was a key part of that strategy.

There has also been concern about the environmental impact of his extensive travels.

Miguel Maduro - a former head of Fifa's governance committee - said Infantino's travelling was "part of his campaign".

He told BBC Sport: "The issue here is that Fifa does not have rules on campaign funding, so the president can claim that he's going there on official business when indirectly he's using that to campaign.

"And anyone else running against him very likely will not have the financial means to compete with that."

Infantino's position seemed vulnerable after it emerged he had been devising a plan to sell stakes in the World Cup and other competitions to private investors, in a proposal called Fifa Forward Enterprise (FFE).

Before FFE was abandoned, Infantino wrote to all 211 member associations saying they would receive $40m (Â£30m) if they backed the plan.

Lars-Christer Olsson - a former chief executive of Uefa - told BBC Sport the "important" question was "what does he promise and what are the conditions, as he already did when he promised cash if the members supported his FFE project?"

BBC Sport gleaned the information about where Infantino had been travelling to, and when, from postings on his social media accounts.

The reasons for his travels include holding meetings with football association presidents, opening pitches, meeting politicians and attending confederation gatherings.

The private jets he used are operated by Qatar Executive (QE) - a subsidiary of the Qatar Airways Group, which is one of Fifa's sponsors.

BBC Sport used flight-tracking records and the jet registrations of the Qatar Executive fleet to compile a detailed log of Infantino's flights, totalling at least 39 since the start of August. Fifa did not dispute the figure.

It said "all travel is in line with Fifa's regulations" and there are "rules which establish the framework for flights and travel by any Fifa official".

But Fifa declined to comment on how the use of QE flights impacts on the money available from the Qatar Airways sponsorship for the benefit of global football.

The governing body also declined to comment when asked when Infantino had last taken a commercial flight.

Olsson said: "What do these trips cost and who is paying, which are the implications for the total football community?

"Is he provided with transport in his private campaign with the idea of getting something back in return if he is elected? If Fifa is paying for private jets, would it not be better to invest this money in the member associations?"

Maduro said: "That's again a confusion between his role as president of Fifa and his role as candidate to Fifa presidency."

He said there were no regulations within Fifa on the "use of public money to support their campaign".

"There's no independent authority that controls that, there is no regulation of campaign funding," he said.

Infantino's trips included attending the Caribbean Football Union presidents' meeting and a junior football tournament in the Dominican Republic three weeks after FFE failed.

He then flew to Paris, where he attended the esports World Cup , alongside French President Emmanuel Macron and Saudi Arabia's Crown Prince Mohammed Bin Salman.

On his Instagram account, Infantino said he had "pledged Fifa's support" for the Pakistan Football Federation president's development plans when he met him in the US in September.

A few days later, he referenced a "wonderful discussion" with the president of the Latvian Football Federation during a visit to Riga, describing him as "my dear friend".

During a visit to Indonesia in October for the final of the Fifa ASEAN Cup, he met presidents from the football federations of Afghanistan, Bangladesh, Brunei Darussalam, Cambodia, Hong Kong, Indonesia, Malaysia, Maldives, Mongolia, Myanmar, Pakistan, Philippines, Qatar, Singapore, Thailand and Timor-Leste.

He said: "Having your support and shared passion on this momentous occasion means a great deal and together we will continue driving this game forward."

And during a recent trip to Manila, he performed on stage as part of a quartet called the 'Backache Boys', alongside officials from the Asian Football Confederation, and the football federations of Malaysia and the Philippines.

Gianni Infantino opened a mini-pitch in Anguilla in November

Gianni Infantino visited the Philippines for the ASEAN Football Federation Ordinary Congress

Gianni Infantino in Qatar to oversee preparations for the Fifa U17 World Cup

Based on our log of Infantino's private-jet flights, we calculated he spent 213 hours and 49 minutes in the air, travelling about 102,870 air miles.

For the type of private jet Infantino was using, this would produce an estimated 1,673 tonnes of CO2e emissions.

That's equivalent to the emissions 252 people would accumulate on average in an entire year, according to European Union data, external. These figures are conservative estimates based on average fuel burn rates.

Fifa has also been criticised for expanding the number of teams at World Cups, and hosting them in multiple countries, thereby increasing the amount of travel. The 2026 World Cup was described as the 'most polluting World Cup ever'.

Freddie Daley, from the Cool Down Sport for Climate Action Network, said Infantino's behaviour was "emblematic of Fifa's approach to environmental sustainability under his leadership: total and utter disregard".

"Despite numerous pledges, Fifa has failed to deliver any significant plan on how it will achieve them and has instead gone backwards," he added.

Maduro said Fifa's support for initiatives on climate action - which included being a signatory of the United Nation's Sports for Climate Action Framework - was "window dressing".

Fifa did not directly respond to a question about its justification for the environmental impact caused by Infantino's extensive travelling.

A spokesperson for Fifa said its objectives were to "improve football constantly and promote it globally".

They added: "The Fifa president has a statutory duty to maintain and develop good relations between and among Fifa, the confederations, 211 member associations, political bodies, and international organisations.

"To do that, he travels around the world and attends Fifa's tournaments and events. Mr Infantino is elected until 18 March 2027, when the next Fifa presidential election will take place.

"Fifa has put in place rules which establish the framework for flights and travel by any Fifa official.

"The Fifa president routinely travels around the world for various duties, together with relevant officials, on business and tournament-related matters and strives to visit member associations of Fifa whenever he can.

"Fifa expenses are published in our annual report, and all travel is in line with Fifa's regulations.

"Fifa's money belongs to the 211 member associations and an increased investment to $6.35bn for the upcoming cycle reinforces our unwavering commitment to further supporting football development and access globally."

Massachusetts sues Trump admin to stop ICE's courthouse arrests: 'Unnecessary fear'

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Massachusetts Attorney General Andrea Campbell filed a federal lawsuit Monday seeking to block ICE from making civil immigration arrests inside state courthouses without a judicial warrant.

Massachusetts officials sued the Trump administration on Monday to block Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) agents from carrying out civil arrests in the state's courthouses, arguing that such arrests without judicial warrants are illegal under state law.

The lawsuit filed by Massachusetts Attorney General Andrea Joy Campbell challenges a 2025 ICE directive that authorizes agents to conduct civil arrests in or near state courthouses without judicial warrants, which she says violates the PROTECT Act, a new state law passed ​by the Democratic-led legislature that prohibits civil immigration arrests in state courthouses without judicial warrants. The courthouse protections took effect Nov. 3.

Campbell said the ICE directive interferes with the state's authority to operate its judicial system, disrupts court proceedings, undermines constitutional principles and public safety and spreads fear, chaos and confusion.

"The Massachusetts PROTECT Act makes it illegal for ICE to conduct civil arrests in our courthouses without a judicial warrant. ICE’s intentional violation of state law has created unnecessary fear, made it more difficult to operate our courts and hold defendants accountable," Campbell said in a statement.

DOJ SUES TO STOP OHIO COURT BLOCKING COURTHOUSE ARRESTS

Massachusetts officials sued the Trump administration to block ICE agents from carrying out civil arrests in the state's courthouses. (Photo by Jim WATSON / AFP via Getty Images)

"ICE is misleading the public by suggesting that these arrests make us safer when, in fact, ICE's actions are preventing criminal cases from moving forward, denying a victim’s ability to obtain accountability, violating a defendant’s due process, and leaving open the possibility that someone facing serious criminal charges could ultimately be released from ICE custody without their criminal case ever being resolved," she continued.

The attorney general said more than 1,110 people have been arrested at her state's courthouses since President Donald Trump returned to the White House in January 2025, including more than 600 people in 2025 and more than 500 during the first 10 months of 2026.

Campbell said ICE has ignored the new state law and arrested at least 38 people in Massachusetts courthouses without judicial warrants in the two and a half weeks since the courthouse protections went into effect.

"I, along with our district attorneys, share the goal of holding defendants accountable for crimes, yet ICE’s unlawful tactics to arrest someone for a civil immigration violation is not the same as holding them accountable for a crime," Campbell said.

FEDERAL JUDGE IN NEW YORK TIES TRUMP ADMIN HANDS ON ARRESTS AT IMMIGRATION COURTS

The attorney general said more than 1,110 people have been arrested at her state's courthouses since President Donald Trump returned to the White House in January 2025. (Getty Images)

Campbell said that ICE's courthouse arrests have continued in Massachusetts despite a federal ruling handed down in June by U.S. District Judge P. Casey Pitts in California that vacated the Trump administration's courthouse arrest policy ​in a separate class action lawsuit.

In Monday's lawsuit, Campbell argues the ICE policy violates the Administrative Procedure Act since it was adopted without taking into account the impact it would have on states and because the Immigration and Nationality Act does not explicitly authorize courthouse arrests.

The lawsuit also alleges that the ⁠policy violates the U.S. Constitution's Tenth Amendment, which protects states' rights to maintain authority over their own judicial systems.

"The Trump Administration’s ICE arrests at Massachusetts courthouses are interfering with our justice system and undermining public safety. When people are afraid to go to court, victims are less likely to report crimes, survivors are less likely to seek protection, and our communities are less safe," Massachusetts Gov. Maura Healey said in a statement. "We expect ICE and all federal agents to follow Massachusetts law."

The lawsuit challenges a 2025 ICE directive that instructs agents to conduct civil arrests in or near state courthouses without judicial warrants. (Bloomberg via Getty Images)

Fox News Digital reached out to the Department of Homeland Security for comment.

In a statement to Reuters, a DHS spokesperson argued that making immigration arrests at courthouses "conserves valuable law enforcement resources because they already ​know where a target will ​be," adding that "the ability of law enforcement ⁠to make arrests of criminal illegal aliens in courthouses is common sense."

A similar lawsuit against ICE arrests at state courthouses was filed in October by California Attorney General Rob Bonta.

Bitcoin Reserves Just Found a New Use on Wall Street, but There’s a Catch

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

Bitcoin reserves have found another potential use within the US financial system. The Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) has clarified that regulated derivatives exchanges and clearinghouses can count Bitcoin, Ethereum and qualifying payment stablecoins toward certain financial resource requirements. However, the regulator is not prepared to treat those holdings like cash.

The guidance, issued on November 23, could give regulated trading venues greater flexibility in managing their balance sheets. For Bitcoin investors, however, the more interesting question is whether that flexibility will translate into fresh institutional demand.

The answer is not quite as straightforward as it might seem.

The CFTC’s latest advisory addresses three types of regulated businesses: swap execution facilities, designated contract markets and derivatives clearing organizations.

These institutions are required to maintain sufficient financial resources to cover at least one year of operating costs. Under the new staff guidance, Bitcoin, Ether and qualifying payment stablecoins may contribute toward those requirements, subject to appropriate valuation discounts.

The announcement follows broader efforts to integrate digital assets into regulated financial infrastructure. NewsBTC previously reported on the CFTC’s guidance concerning tokenized collateral at clearinghouses, another area where traditional financial markets are gradually accommodating blockchain-based assets.

This time, the discussion is not about what traders can post as collateral. It is about the assets that the institutions running those markets can rely on to keep their businesses operating.

For a company that already holds Bitcoin, the clarification could make those holdings more useful from a regulatory standpoint. For businesses considering adding cryptocurrency to their balance sheets, it removes some uncertainty surrounding how those assets would be recognized.

But that recognition comes at a discount!

The CFTC advisory references a minimum 20% valuation haircut for Bitcoin and Ethereum, consistent with earlier staff treatment of proprietary cryptocurrency positions held by futures commission merchants.

Qualifying payment stablecoins receive considerably more favorable treatment, with a referenced haircut of 2%.

An exchange holding $10 million in Bitcoin, for example, would be able to count $8 million toward the relevant financial resource requirement if it applied a 20% haircut and no further deductions were necessary.

The remaining $2 million is not a realized loss. It is a regulatory adjustment intended to account for the risk associated with holding a volatile asset.

Crypto assets that are neither readily marketable nor qualifying payment stablecoins would generally face a 100% haircut, effectively excluding them from the calculation.

Perhaps the most revealing part of the advisory is the regulator’s warning about what it calls wrong-way risk.

A DCM should consider potential wrong-way risk

For a cryptocurrency-focused exchange, that risk is particularly relevant. A prolonged Bitcoin sell-off could put pressure on trading revenues while simultaneously reducing the value of the assets the exchange relies on to support its operations.

The problem is not simply that Bitcoin is volatile. It is that its value could deteriorate at precisely the moment a crypto business finds itself under financial pressure.

A 20% valuation discount provides some protection against that scenario, but it is hardly a guarantee.

Despite allowing digital assets to contribute toward broader operating resources, the CFTC has maintained a separate requirement for cash and highly liquid securities.

Bitcoin, Ethereum and even qualifying payment stablecoins cannot be used to satisfy that particular obligation under the advisory.

Swap execution facilities must maintain qualifying liquid resources sufficient to cover at least three months of projected operating expenses or the cost of winding down their operations, whichever is greater.

For designated contract markets and derivatives clearing organizations, the operating-cost liquidity requirement generally covers at least six months.

The distinction matters because an institution can have substantial assets on its balance sheet without necessarily having the cash available to meet immediate obligations.

It is also worth noting that the guidance does not address whether cryptocurrencies can satisfy the separate financial resources that clearinghouses must maintain to cover clearing-member defaults.

In effect, the CFTC is recognizing Bitcoin as an asset that can contribute to an institution’s financial strength without giving it the same regulatory standing as cash.

That is a more cautious approach than some Bitcoin advocates might have hoped for, but arguably a more sustainable one for integrating cryptocurrency into financial infrastructure.

The bullish interpretation is relatively simple. If regulated financial institutions can derive greater utility from holding Bitcoin, more of them may eventually consider allocating capital to the cryptocurrency.

The expansion of regulated crypto infrastructure has already brought digital assets closer to traditional financial markets. Earlier, NewsBTC reported on Coinbase’s CFTC registration for its USDC-focused derivatives clearinghouse, illustrating how cryptocurrency businesses are increasingly operating within established regulatory frameworks.

The latest advisory gives relevant institutions another reason to evaluate how digital assets fit into their balance sheets.

Still, there is a considerable difference between allowing an institution to hold Bitcoin for regulatory purposes and giving it an economic incentive to buy more.

An exchange that already owns Bitcoin could benefit from the clarification without purchasing a single additional coin. Another might conclude that holding conventional securities remains more efficient, particularly given the valuation discounts and separate liquidity requirements.

Nothing in the advisory requires institutions to acquire Bitcoin, and no purchase commitments were disclosed alongside the announcement.

Investors should therefore resist the temptation to translate this development directly into a bullish price forecast.

The longer-term significance may be greater than the immediate market impact. Every additional recognized use for Bitcoin within regulated financial markets strengthens the case for its utility beyond speculative trading.

However, the November 23 document remains a staff advisory rather than a binding rule adopted by the full Commission. It does not amend existing regulations or create enforceable rights.

The next meaningful development will be evidence that regulated institutions are actually changing their reserve allocations in response.

Until then, Bitcoin has gained another form of regulatory recognition. Whether it gains another source of sustained buying pressure is a different question entirely.

Claude can now write, review and approve its own code — here's why that's a big deal

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This is a major upgrade and worth exploring whether you regularly vibe code or are just starting out

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Anthropic just introduced Claude Ship, a new AI tool that takes Claude's coding capabilities a step further. Instead of simply writing code, Claude can now review it, find problems, make corrections and even approve changes without requiring someone to supervise every step. So whether you're developing an app or creating a website, Claude will check its own work to ensure you're crafting the best design. Just announced Monday, Claude Ship is designed to tackle one of the more frustrating parts of software development.

But writing code is only part of what it can do. Before an update can go live, someone typically needs to review it, check for errors and approve the changes.

Claude can now handle much of that process automatically. For developers, that could mean spending less time reviewing code and more time building new features. But even for people experimenting with AI to create their own apps or websites, it's another example of how quickly Claude is becoming capable of doing work that once required multiple people.

Claude Ship combines three AI-powered tools that work together to move software updates through the approval process.

The first, Code Review, examines proposed changes and looks for potential bugs or other problems. It was already available before today's announcement.

The second, PR Steward, is new and takes things further. When Claude finds an issue, PR Steward can work on fixing it rather than simply alerting the developer. It can also respond to failed automated tests, conflicting code changes and feedback from human reviewers.

Finally, Claude Approvals determines whether a change is ready to be approved.

Sign up to the Tom's AI Guide weekly newsletter summing up all the biggest AI news you need to know. Plus, analysis from our AI editors and tips on how to use the latest AI tools!

There's an important catch; Claude doesn't get unlimited authority to approve whatever it wants. That's a good thing! Anyone using this feature can establish their own rules about which changes AI can approve and which require a human reviewer.

For example, a small documentation correction might be approved automatically, while a change involving sensitive financial information could still require human oversight.

Developers can also tell Claude to stop working on a particular task even if it was not listed as needing approval previously.

Arguably the most interesting part of the announcement is how anyone can use Claude Ship directly from Claude Code.

A new /ship command starts the process. Claude can push the changes to GitHub, open a pull request, review the code and address problems that come up along the way.

A pull request is essentially a proposed update to a software project. Normally, it needs to pass through several checks before becoming part of the finished product.

Rather than having to return repeatedly to fix problems or respond to comments, developers can leave Claude working on those tasks while they move on to something else.

Anthropic says the technology has already changed how its own Claude Code team works.

According to the company, the number of pull requests merged by the team more than doubled between June and September, while the share of those changes that later needed to be reverted fell by more than 40%.

Those are Anthropic's internal results, however, and they don't guarantee other teams will see the same improvements.

Like most advanced Claude features, Claude Ship will first roll out to Claude Team customers working with GitHub repositories. However, the existing Code Review feature is generally available, while PR Steward and Claude Approvals remain in beta.This isn't a feature that someone using the free Claude chatbot can immediately try. So if you don't have a Team or Enterprise subscription, this is a good time to discover more about what Claude Code can do. Because vibe coding has become so integrated into many workflows, the more you can practice and feel comfortable with it, the more productive you might be.

Usage is billed according to the amount of AI processing required, so the cost can vary depending on how many changes Claude reviews and how much work it performs. Organizations can establish spending limits to keep costs under control.

Claude Ship is another sign of how AI coding tools are evolving beyond assistants that simply respond to instructions.

Instead of asking Claude to write a piece of code and then taking over, developers can increasingly hand off larger portions of the software development process.

Don't let the possibilities of this new feature fool you; this doesn't eliminate the need for human oversight. Anthropic acknowledges that Claude Ship can still miss bugs, security vulnerabilities and other problems, making careful approval policies important.

Still, the direction is clear. AI is moving from helping people write software to taking responsibility for more of the work required to finish it. For everyday users, it's worth keeping an eye out for this feature to eventually come to lower subscription tiers.
